Question 1: question_1

❓ Question

How do I request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/TensorFlow/Example4/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/gres.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise3/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise1/run_4.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise2/run_2.sbatch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To request a single GPU on FASRC Cannon, add the following line to your SLURM submission script: ```bash #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 ``` You'll also need to specify the GPU partition and request at least one node: ```bash #SBATCH --partition=gpu #SBATCH --nodes=1 ``` A minimal example: ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --job-name=my_gpu_job #SBATCH --partition=gpu #SBATCH --nodes=1 #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 #SBATCH --time=00:30:00 #SBATCH --mem=8G # Restrict CUDA to the GPU allocated by SLURM export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$SLURM_JOB_GPUS # Run your command your_gpu_command ``` For more details on GPU resources, see the FASRC documentation on [Running Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/).
✅ Expected Answer
Add #SBATCH --gpus=1 to your submission script and SLURM will give the job access to one GPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 3: question_3

❓ Question

How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
By default, a SLURM job on Cannon gets **100 MB per node** if you don't specify a memory request. This is a deliberately small limit, and if your job exceeds it, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. To request more memory, add this line to your job script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the amount in MB (e.g., `--mem 4000` for 4 GB, or `--mem 4G`). For guidance on sizing your request, you can use the `jobstats` command to check how much memory your job actually uses, then set `--mem` slightly higher than that peak value.
✅ Expected Answer
If you omit the memory request you are granted 100 MB by default, which is usually too little and will likely cause the job to be killed for going over.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
💬 Agent Messages (3)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
By default, a SLURM job on Cannon gets **100 MB per node** if you don't specify a memory request. This is a deliberately small limit, and if your job exceeds it, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. To request more memory, add this line to your job script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the amount in MB (e.g., `--mem 4000` for 4 GB, or `--mem 4G`). For guidance on sizing your request, you can use the `jobstats` command to check how much memory your job actually uses, then set `--mem` slightly higher than that peak value.

Question 4: question_4

❓ Question

What is the storage quota for a FASRC user home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The FASRC home directory has a **100 GB hard quota** and a **95 GB soft quota** that triggers notifications when exceeded [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). If you go over the 95 GB soft quota, you'll receive an email notification. If you exceed the 100 GB hard quota, you'll get a "no space left on device" error and won't be able to write new files until you reduce your usage below the hard limit [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). You can check your current usage with the `quota` command: ```bash quota $HOME ``` [Checking quota and usage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage/)
✅ Expected Answer
Each user gets a 100 GB home directory, enforced as a hard limit.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: What is the storage quota for a FASRC user home directory?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
The FASRC home directory has a **100 GB hard quota** and a **95 GB soft quota** that triggers notifications when exceeded [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). If you go over the 95 GB soft quota, you'll receive an email notification. If you exceed the 100 GB hard quota, you'll get a "no space left on device" error and won't be able to write new files until you reduce your usage below the hard limit [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/). You can check your current usage with the `quota` command: ```bash quota $HOME ``` [Checking quota and usage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage/)

Question 5: question_5

❓ Question

How much storage does a FASRC lab group directory get, and how many files?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, a **Lab Directory** (`/n/holylabs`) provides: * **Storage:** 4TB (this cannot be expanded) * **Retention:** Allowed (no automatic deletion based on age) * **Backup:** No backup (no snapshots) The documentation does not specify a limit on the **number of files** for the Lab Directory. For comparison, FASRC also offers **netscratch** (`/n/netscratch`) with 50TB of storage, but it has a 90-day retention policy and no backup. If you need more storage than the 4TB Lab Directory provides, FASRC offers paid storage options, including Compute Storage and Lab Storage. For more details, see the [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Each lab gets a 4 TiB lab directory as a hard limit, with a 1 million file limit.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

🤖 Assistant Message #2

For comparison, FASRC also offers **netscratch** (`/n/netscratch`) with 50TB of storage, but it has a 90-day retention policy and no backup. If you need more storage than the 4TB Lab Directory provides, FASRC offers paid storage options, including Compute Storage and Lab Storage. ❓ Question

What command lists the modules I currently have loaded on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/editing-your-bashrc

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To list the modules you currently have loaded on Cannon, run: ```bash module list ``` This command shows all modules that are currently active in your shell session. You can also use `module avail` to see what modules are available to load, and `module purge` to unload all currently loaded modules. See the [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/) documentation for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
Run module list to display all modules currently loaded in your shell environment.

❓ Question

What command shows my lab's fairshare information on FASRC?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/dual-lab-affiliations-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/kempner-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-stats, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/fair_tree.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sshare.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, slurm.schedmd.com/qos.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The command to check your lab's fairshare score is: ```bash sshare -u ``` This will display your fairshare information, including your effective score in the last column. As a rule of thumb, a fairshare score of ≤ 0.5 indicates lower priority, while a score ≥ 0.5 indicates higher priority for job scheduling [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/). For more detailed fairshare information and calculations, you can also use the `scalc` command, which performs various SLURM calculations including projected fairshare usage [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/).
✅ Expected Answer
Use sshare (e.g. sshare --account= -a) to see RawShares, NormShares, RawUsage, EffectvUsage, and the FairShare score for an account.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 10: question_10

❓ Question

How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The home directories are backed up daily, and you can access older snapshots via the hidden `.snapshot` directory in your home folder. Keep in mind that if you delete your entire home directory, the snapshots are also deleted and are not recoverable [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, accessible via the .snapshot directory.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage
💬 Agent Messages (3)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document

Question 11: question_11

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed for running out of memory. How do I find out how much memory it actually used, and how do I request more next time?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's how to find out how much memory your job used and how to request more next time: ## How much memory did your job actually use? 1. **Use `jobstats`** to see the peak memory usage of your job: ```bash jobstats JOBID ``` Replace `JOBID` with your actual job ID. This tool shows the current memory usage of a running job or the memory usage of a completed job. **Important caveat:** If your job terminated due to being `OUT_OF_MEMORY`, the memory usage recorded by Slurm will be **inaccurate**. To get an accurate measurement, you need a job that completes successfully, as Slurm will then record the true memory peak [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). 2. **Check job state** to confirm it was killed for memory: ```bash sacct -j JOBID ``` Look for `OUT_OF_ME+` in the State column, which indicates the job exceeded the memory limit [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). ## How to request more memory next time Add this line to your job submission script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` Where `X` is the maximum amount of memory your job will use **per node**, in MB. You can also use GB units by adding `G`: ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G ``` ### Tips for choosing the right amount: - Start with a larger value (job slots average about 4000 MB per core, though most jobs need less) - Use `jobstats` to see how much your job actually uses - Set `--mem` to something slightly larger than what `jobstats` reports, since it defines a hard upper limit - Requesting smaller memory amounts makes it easier for the scheduler to find a place to run your job [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/) ### For multi-node jobs: The memory value is the maximum used on **any one node**. If you're not setting an even distribution of tasks per node (e.g., with `--ntasks-per-node`), the same job could have very different memory values when run at different times [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/).
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j --format=JobID,Elapsed,ReqMem,MaxRSS to compare the peak memory used (MaxRSS) against what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script raise the request with --mem=G (whole job) or --mem-per-cpu=G, setting it a bit above the observed MaxRSS so normal variation doesn't kill the job.

Question 12: question_12

❓ Question

Is the two-factor code for logging into FASRC the same as my Harvard Key code? If not, how do I set it up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the two-factor code for logging into FASRC is **not the same** as your Harvard Key code. They are separate tokens managed through different systems, though you can use the same Duo Mobile app to display both. ### How to set up your RC two-factor code in Duo Mobile If you already have Duo Mobile installed, you can add your RC token by following these steps: 1. **Get the RC QR Code** (from a computer or tablet, not your phone): - Visit [https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://two-factor.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) and log in with your RC username and password. - Check your email for a link, then click it to display a page with your OpenAuth token in QR code format. Leave this page open. 2. **Add the token to Duo Mobile** (on your phone): - Open the Duo Mobile app and tap the **+** icon to add a new token. - Use your phone's camera to scan the QR code displayed on your computer screen. - Confirm the addition when prompted. Once added, you can tap the key icon next to your RC token entry in Duo Mobile to view the 6-digit code. This code works for logging into the cluster, connecting to the RC VPN, and other RC services [Using Duo Mobile to display your RC two-factor code](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile/).
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC has its own two-factor system (OpenAuth) that is separate from Harvard Key. Set it up with a smartphone authenticator app (e.g. Google Authenticator or Duo) before accessing cluster resources; you use this OpenAuth token along with your FASRC username and password.

Question 13: question_13

❓ Question

How do I find which versions of a software package are available as modules on Cannon, and how should I load it so my workflow keeps working when defaults change?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/pbdMPI/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/training-materials, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's how to find and load software modules on Cannon: ## Finding Available Versions Use these commands to discover what versions are available: - **`module avail`** — Lists modules available in your current environment. Note that loading certain modules (like compilers or MPI) may unlock additional dependent modules due to Lmod's hierarchy. - **`module --default avail`** — Shows the full list regardless of your current environment state. - **`module spider `** — Provides detailed information about a specific package, including all available versions and dependencies. ## Loading Modules Safely **Always specify the full module name with version** in your scripts and workflows: ```bash module load matlab/R2025b-fasrc01 module load gcc/15.2.0-fasrc01 module load openmpi/5.0.8-fasrc01 ``` **Do not rely on short names** like `module load matlab`, which will load whatever version is set as the default. The default can change based on alphanumeric sorting or FASRC's choice of the most stable/common version. As the documentation states: *"It is strongly recommended to load modules specifying name and specific version. This way you are protected from default changes."* [Lmod software modules](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/) ## Useful Module Commands - **`module list`** — Shows currently loaded modules - **`module purge`** — Unloads all loaded modules - **`module save `** — Saves your current module set as a named collection - **`module restore `** — Restores a previously saved collection This approach ensures your workflow remains reproducible even when FASRC updates default module versions.
✅ Expected Answer
Search with module spider to list all available modules matching the string regardless of your current environment. Then load with both the name and a specific version (module load /) — FASRC strongly recommends this so you are protected if the default version changes later.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Question 14: question_14

❓ Question

What's the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment for a project on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cursor-remote-development-via-ssh-and-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment on Cannon is to use **Mamba** (a faster drop-in replacement for Conda) inside a virtual environment. Direct use of `pip` outside of a virtual environment is discouraged on FASRC clusters. Here is the recommended workflow: 1. **Load the Python module** (which provides Mamba): ```bash module load python ``` 2. **Start an interactive session** before creating or modifying environments: ```bash salloc --partition test --nodes=1 --cpus-per-task=2 --mem=4GB --time=0-02:00:00 ``` 3. **Create an environment** (default location is `~/.conda/envs`): ```bash mamba create -n my_project_env python=3.9 numpy pandas ``` *You can specify the Python version and packages directly in the creation command to ensure dependencies are met.* 4. **Activate the environment**: ```bash source activate my_project_env ``` 5. **Install additional packages** as needed: ```bash mamba install -y ``` 6. **Deactivate** when finished: ```bash source deactivate ``` **Important Notes:** * **Channels:** The FASRC `python` module is built with Miniforge3 and uses `conda-forge` as the default channel. The default Anaconda channel (`repo.anaconda.com`) is blocked on the cluster due to licensing restrictions. * **Custom Locations:** If you want to store packages in a Lab shared directory instead of your home directory, you can set environment variables before creating the environment: ```bash export CONDA_PKGS_DIRS=//conda/pkgs export CONDA_ENVS_PATH=//conda/envs ``` For more details, see the [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation) guide.
✅ Expected Answer
Load a module that provides mamba (python/3*, Miniforge3, or Mambaforge), then create an isolated environment, e.g. mamba create -n data_env python=3.9 pandas numpy, and activate it with source activate data_env. FASRC recommends using Miniforge with mamba so dependencies are isolated and you avoid conflicts rather than working in the base environment.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 15: question_15

❓ Question

How is my job's priority determined on Cannon, and how can I see the priority of my pending jobs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
### How Job Priority Is Determined on Cannon Job priority on Cannon is calculated using two main components, based on Slurm's FairShare and multifactor priority system: 1. **FairShare Score:** This is the dominant factor. Every research lab (account) has a "Share" of the cluster resources. The FairShare score compares what the lab has been granted (Share) against what it has actually used (Usage). * Labs that have **not** used their full allocation get a higher FairShare score, which translates to higher job priority. * Labs that have **overused** their allocation get a lower score, reducing their job priority. * This score is multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to create the "FairShare Priority" component. * Usage is tracked using Trackable RESources (TRES) for CPU, GPU, and Memory, normalized to account for different hardware architectures. [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare) 2. **Job Age:** This component accrues over time. The longer a job has been waiting in the pending queue, the more priority it gains. This ensures that jobs don't wait indefinitely. [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare) The final job priority is a combination of these two factors, which determines the job's position in the pending queue relative to other jobs. ### How to See the Priority of Your Pending Jobs You can view the priority of your jobs using the `squeue` command. To specifically see the priority column for your pending jobs, you can use: ```bash squeue -u $USER -o "%i %j %p %T %P" ``` * `%i`: Job ID * `%j`: Job Name * `%p`: Partition * `%T`: Job State (PD = Pending, R = Running, etc.) * `%P`: Job Priority You can also sort by priority to see which of your pending jobs has the highest priority first: ```bash squeue -u $USER -o "%i %j %p %T %P" --sort=-P ``` This will list your pending jobs sorted by priority in descending order (highest priority first).
✅ Expected Answer
A job's priority combines two parts: your FairShare score multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to make it an integer, plus job age, which accrues while the job waits and caps at 3 days on Cannon. Inspect the breakdown for pending jobs with sprio, or use showq -o -p to see the pending queue ordered by priority.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

❓ Question

I need to move a very large dataset to the cluster. Should I use Globus, and can I transfer it straight into my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/transferring-data-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**Yes, use Globus** for very large datasets — it's specifically recommended for large transfers and supports resuming interrupted transfers, which is much more reliable than SCP/SSH for big data [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). **No, you cannot transfer directly to your home directory.** Home directories are explicitly excluded from Globus access for security reasons (to prevent accidental sharing of authentication keys/passwords) [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). Instead, transfer your data to one of these locations: - **Lab directory** (`/n/holylabs`) — 4TB complimentary, no retention limit - **Netscratch** (`/n/netscratch`) — 50TB complimentary, 90-day retention policy - **Lab Storage** (paid) — for longer-term retention When using Globus, you can only access folders inside `Lab` or `Users/$USER` within your lab share [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). Would you like help setting up Globus access or choosing the right storage location for your dataset?
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, use Globus — it's the recommended alternative to SSH/SCP specifically for very large data sets, and you can install Globus Connect Personal to make your own machine an endpoint. However, you cannot transfer into your home directory: home directories are not available through Globus (and never will be) because sharing them is a security vulnerability. Target a lab or scratch location instead.

Question 17: question_17

❓ Question

I accidentally deleted some files in my home directory on Cannon. Can I get them back, and is there any situation where I can't?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/R/Rstan/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmctld.html, slurm.schedmd.com/strigger.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's what you need to know about recovering deleted files from your home directory on Cannon: ### Yes, recovery is often possible Your home directory is **regularly backed up** [User Quick Start Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide/). This means that in many cases, accidentally deleted files can be restored from these backups. ### When recovery may NOT be possible While the documentation confirms home directories are backed up, it does not provide specific details about: - The backup frequency or retention period - The exact process for requesting a restore - Any time limits or conditions under which recovery would fail ### What to do next Since the documentation doesn't outline the specific recovery procedure, you should: 1. **Contact FASRC support** directly to request a file restore from your home directory backup 2. Reach out through the [FASRC portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/rcrt/tickets/) to submit a ticket ### Important note about lab directories Be aware that **lab directory backups** are for system-wide disaster recovery only and do not have snapshot capabilities—they are not intended to recover accidental file deletions [Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/). However, this restriction applies to lab directories, not your personal home directory. Would you like help finding the contact information for FASRC support?
✅ Expected Answer
Usually yes: home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, so you can recover recently deleted files from the .snapshot directory. The exception is if you delete your entire home directory — that also deletes the snapshots, and it is not recoverable.

It executed multiple hybrid vector searches targeting keywords like "Cannon GPU job request," "SLURM memory OOM killed," and general GPU computing documentation. **Best Possible Answer** Based on the retrieved context and standard SLURM practices for the Cannon cluster: 1. **Requesting the GPU:** Use the `--gres=gpu:1` (or appropriate number) flag in your `sbatch` script or `srun` command. Ensure you are submitting to the correct partition (e.g., `gpu` or `test` depending on your job type and quota). 2. **Preventing OOM Kills:** To ensure your job isn't killed due to memory limits, you must explicitly request sufficient memory using the `--mem` or `--mem-per-gpu` flag. * **Per-GPU Memory:** Use `--mem-per-gpu=XXG` (e.g., `--mem-per-gpu=30G`) to allocate memory relative to the number of GPUs requested. This is often safer than total node memory for GPU jobs. * **Total Memory:** Alternatively, use `--mem=XXG` to specify total memory for the job. * **Check Defaults:** If you do not specify memory, SLURM may assign a default limit that is too low for your workload, leading to OOM kills. *Example Snippet:* ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1 # Request 1 GPU #SBATCH --mem-per-gpu=30G # Request 30GB memory per GPU #SBATCH -t 01:00:00 # Time limit ``` **Note on Execution** This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50. The agent was unable to perform further iterative searches or tool calls to retrieve the exact default memory limits or specific partition names for Cannon, so please verify the specific partition names and default memory caps in the FASRC Cannon documentation if the above generic SLURM flags do not meet your specific quota requirements.
✅ Expected Answer
Request the GPU with #SBATCH --gpus=1. Don't rely on the default memory — if you omit a memory request you only get 100 MB and the job will likely be killed, so set #SBATCH --mem=G to a value your job needs. Submit to a GPU-capable partition and keep within its time limit (for short tests, use a test/short partition).

❓ Question

I'm Harvard faculty but I just want to run jobs inside a colleague's existing lab group on the cluster — I don't want my own group and I don't want to deal with billing. Which job title should I pick on the account request form?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mcz-staff-compute

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You should select **POSTDOC/RESEARCHER** on the account request form. According to the FASRC account qualifications documentation, "Harvard faculty who are simply collaborating with another PI should also use this designation to avoid triggering billing and vetting required for a new cluster lab group" [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). This allows you to run jobs under your colleague's existing lab group without creating a new group or triggering the billing/vetting process associated with a FACULTY designation.
✅ Expected Answer
Pick POSTDOC/RESEARCHER. A Harvard faculty member who wants to sign up as an individual collaborator under another faculty member's cluster group should choose POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, because choosing FACULTY requires billing information and starts the new lab setup process (and FACULTY signups require vetting, so they take longer to process).

Question 23: question_23

❓ Question

What does someone with non-faculty PI rights have to supply at signup that an ordinary researcher does not?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC Account Qualifications documentation, someone with **non-faculty PI rights** must supply the following at signup that an ordinary researcher (POSTDOC/RESEARCHER) does not: 1. **A supported affiliation** – You must have a PI affiliation that is supported by FASRC. 2. **A 33-digit billing string** – You must provide your own billing code(s) to cover cluster usage charges. Additionally, your account and PI rights will be **vetted before approval**, and FASRC may contact you for more information during this process. In contrast, ordinary researchers (POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, GRAD STUDENT, UNDERGRAD, or STAFF) are sponsored by a PI who already has these qualifications and do not need to provide their own billing strings or undergo PI-level vetting. Source: [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/)
✅ Expected Answer
A NON-FACULTY PI must supply a 33-digit billing code at signup. Non-Faculty PI accounts also carry additional controls and privileges, and the signup requires vetting so it takes longer to process.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Question 24: question_24

❓ Question

Our lab has grant money and wants to buy extra compute nodes for the cluster. Can we just have them racked whenever we're ready?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/jupyter-notebook-server-on-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/SLUG23/NERSC-SLUG23.pdf

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you cannot simply rack and add compute nodes to the FASRC cluster on your own. The FASRC cluster is a large, shared institutional resource, and hardware procurement, rack deployment, and integration are managed centrally by FASRC infrastructure teams—not by individual labs or PIs. While your lab may have grant funds available for computing resources, adding physical hardware to the cluster involves: - **Centralized procurement and specifications** – FASRC ensures new nodes meet the cluster’s hardware, firmware, and networking standards. - **Facilities and rack space** – Adding nodes requires coordination with Harvard Facilities for power, cooling, and physical rack space. - **Integration and configuration** – New nodes must be integrated into the SLURM workload manager, storage networks, monitoring, and security infrastructure. If you're interested in expanding your lab's compute capacity, your best options are: 1. **Request additional allocation** on the existing cluster through the standard allocation process. 2. **Explore cloud computing** (e.g., AWS, GCP, Azure) through Harvard's institutional agreements, which can be provisioned quickly using grant funds. 3. **Contact FASRC directly** to discuss whether there are any pathways for sponsored hardware donations or partnerships. For more information on cluster access and allocations, see the [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/) page.
✅ Expected Answer
No. FASRC will discuss your computing needs, coordinate a quote, and install the new hardware, but as of the 2019 cluster refresh and the move to water-cooling, new nodes can be added into the cluster only at certain times of the year.
🎯 Expected Source Documents

No, you cannot simply rack and add compute nodes to the FASRC cluster on your own. The FASRC cluster is a large, shared institutional resource, and hardware procurement, rack deployment, and integration are managed centrally by FASRC infrastructure teams—not by individual labs or PIs. While your lab may have grant funds available for computing resources, adding physical hardware to the cluster involves: - **Centralized procurement and specifications** – FASRC ensures new nodes meet the cluster’s hardware, firmware, and networking standards. - **Facilities and rack space** – Adding nodes requires coordination with Harvard Facilities for power, cooling, and physical rack space. - **Integration and configuration** – New nodes must be integrated into the SLURM workload manager, storage networks, monitoring, and security infrastructure. If you're interested in expanding your lab's compute capacity, your best options are: 1. **Request additional allocation** on the existing cluster through the standard allocation process. 2. **Explore cloud computing** (e.g., AWS, GCP, Azure) through Harvard's institutional agreements, which can be provisioned quickly using grant funds. 3. **Contact FASRC directly** to discuss whether there are any pathways for sponsored hardware donations or partnerships. For more information on cluster access and allocations, see the [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/) page.

❓ Question

If a Chan School PI sponsors an outside collaborator's cluster account, who ends up paying for that person's compute time, and what happens to their access if the PI leaves?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation for the Chan School of Public Health: **Billing:** There is no up-front cost for the outside collaborator's account, but their compute usage (CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and **billed to the Chan School on a quarterly basis** [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/). **If the PI leaves:** The collaborator's access **ends** unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing. It is the departing PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/).
✅ Expected Answer
There is no up-front cost for the collaborator's account, but their cluster usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school on a quarterly basis. The account must be requested as EXTERNAL and be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account. Should the PI leave HCSPH, the collaborator's access also ends unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing; it is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.

How long can a job run on the Chan School's own GPU nodes, and what GPUs are actually in them?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Based on the [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview/), the Chan School has its own dedicated GPU partition called **`hsph_gpu`** with the following specifications: **GPU hardware:** - **8 Nvidia H200 GPUs** per partition (4 per node, 2 nodes) - Each GPU node has 64 AMD Genoa cores and 1.5TB of RAM - Nodes are water-cooled and interconnected with NDR InfiniBand **Time limit:** - **3 days** (72 hours) So a job on the Chan School's GPU nodes can run for up to **3 days**, and the GPUs are **Nvidia H200** models.
✅ Expected Answer
The hsph_gpu partition has a 3 day time limit. The block contains 128 AMD Genoa cores and 8 Nvidia H200 GPUs; each node is water-cooled with 64 cores, 1.5TB of RAM, and 4 GPUs, and the nodes are interconnected with NDR Infiniband. (The CPU-only hsph partition also has a 3 day time limit.)

Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview/), **no, the annual per-user fee is no longer charged.** The document states: > The previous annual per-user charge for FAS Research Computing accounts is *no longer* used as of the fiscal year 2019. Standard services, including accounts and access to the FAS RC high-performance computing cluster, will be effectively free to all Harvard Chan School community members via the account of an eligible PI. These FASRC computing costs are now covered by the school's overhead rate. So as of FY2019, there is no longer a yearly per-person fee just to have an account. Standard account access is covered by the school's overhead. However, supplemental resources like increased storage and VMs may still involve additional charges.

Supplemental resources such as increased storage and VMs still involve additional charges.

Question 28: question_28

❓ Question

I'm on FASSE and want to run VS Code on a compute node. Can I just set up Remote-SSH to the compute node the way I do on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you can't just use the same Remote-SSH setup you use on Cannon for FASSE. The FASRC documentation specifically covers setting up VS Code on **Cannon**, not FASSE, and the two clusters have different architectures and login nodes. Here's what you need to know: ### Why it's different - The Cannon setup uses a `ProxyCommand` that tunnels through Cannon's login node (`cannon`) to allocate a compute node via `salloc` and then connects via `nc` to port 22. This is cluster-specific and won't work for FASSE. - FASSE has different login nodes and potentially different networking. ### Recommended approach for FASSE FASRC recommends using **Approach I: Remote – Tunnel via batch job** for resilience against network glitches. This involves: 1. Creating a batch job script (`vscode.job`) that: - Submits to SLURM with your desired resources (`--partition`, `--mem`, `--time`, etc.) - Downloads and extracts the VS Code CLI - Launches a VS Code tunnel using `code tunnel --accept-server-license-terms --name ` 2. Submitting this job via `sbatch` from the FASSE login node 3. Connecting from your local VS Code using the Remote – Tunnel extension ### Important notes - You're limited to **5 login sessions** total, so be mindful of how many VS Code instances you spawn [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/) - Harvard users should use **GitHub authentication** for the VS Code tunnel (Microsoft auth doesn't work with Harvard Microsoft accounts) - You'll need to adjust the SLURM directives (`--partition`, `--mem`, `--time`, etc.) based on your FASSE workflow Would you like me to help you adapt the batch job script for FASSE's specific resources and partitions?
✅ Expected Answer
No. You cannot use Remote SSH to connect your local VS Code to a FASSE compute node, because salloc is not permitted on FASSE for security reasons. Remote SSH to FASSE's login node does work. For a FASSE compute node, launching a Remote Tunnel as a batch job (Approach I) is your only option — and before doing that you must enable internet access by setting a network proxy (you are on the FASSE VPN realm, @fasse), including adding the same proxy in your local VS Code settings; remember to unset that proxy in VS Code when you are not on the FASSE VPN, or it will block your other non-FASSE VS Code remote sessions.
🎯 Expected Source Documents

Question 29: question_29

❓ Question

The VS Code Dev Containers extension keeps trying to talk to a Docker daemon on the cluster, which doesn't exist there. What do I have to change so it uses the container runtime FASRC actually provides?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC uses **Podman** (a daemonless container runtime), not Docker, so the Dev Containers extension can't find a Docker daemon on the cluster. To make it work, you need to: ### 1. Start a Podman system service on the compute node In a VS Code terminal on the cluster, run: ```bash nohup podman system service -t 0 >/dev/null 2>&1 & ``` This starts a Podman API service listening on a Unix socket (typically `/tmp/containers-user-$(id -u)/podman/podman.sock`). ### 2. Update your `devcontainer.json` Add the following settings to bind-mount the Podman socket into the container and disable Docker BuildKit: ```json { "remoteUser": "root", "mounts": [ "source=/tmp/podman-run-/podman/podman.sock,target=/var/run/docker-host.sock,type=bind" ], "remoteEnv": { "DOCKER_BUILDKIT": "0" }, "workspaceFolder": "${localWorkspaceFolder}", "workspaceMount": "source=${localWorkspaceFolder},target=${localWorkspaceFolder},type=bind" } ``` Replace `` with your actual user ID (e.g., `1234`). ### Why this works FASRC provides **Podman** as its container runtime — the `docker` command on the cluster is actually a wrapper that calls `podman` under the hood [Podman – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman). However, Podman is daemonless, so there's no background Docker/Podman daemon to connect to. The `podman system service` command creates a temporary API socket that tools like Dev Containers can use as a drop-in replacement for the Docker socket [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel). > **Note:** The service only lasts for the lifetime of the compute node session. If you reconnect to a different node, you'll need to restart the service.
✅ Expected Answer
Point the Dev Containers extension at Podman. In Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers, change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Path' from 'docker' to 'podman', and change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path' from '/var/run/docker.sock' to '/tmp/podman-run-/podman/podman.sock', replacing with your FASRC user ID (obtained by running 'id -u' on the cluster). If you are using a local VS Code rather than an OOD Remote Desktop, you will need to revert these settings later if you use dev containers with a local Docker installation.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 30: question_30

❓ Question

I set up TF 2.16 on the cluster with the FASRC conda steps and now Keras blows up saying it has no backend. What environment variable am I supposed to export?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/TensorFlow/Example2/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You need to export: ```bash export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" ``` Starting with TensorFlow 2.16.1, Keras 3.0 is included, and it requires this environment variable to be set. You can add it to your conda environment's activation script so it's set automatically each time you activate the environment: ```bash mkdir -p $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d echo 'export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"' >> $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d/env_vars.sh ``` See the FASRC TensorFlow documentation for full setup details [TensorFlow – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow/).
✅ Expected Answer
Export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow". Starting with version 2.16.1, TensorFlow includes Keras 3.0, so the Keras backend must be set to TF. FASRC's instructions include `export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"` in both the GPU and CPU conda recipes, and prefix the singularity runs with it as well, e.g. `KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" singularity exec --nv tf2.16.1_gpu.simg python3`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

In FASRC's GPU conda recipe for TensorFlow, which CUDA toolkit and cuDNN versions are pinned?

Question 32: question_32

❓ Question

My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the `gpu_test` partition, GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the physical card into smaller instances. To use the GPU correctly with PyTorch, you need to explicitly set the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable to the MIG instance ID. Here's what to do: 1. **Find the MIG instance name:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set the environment variable:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG- ``` Or automate it: ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This is **not required** on the regular `gpu` partition, only on `gpu_test`. See the PyTorch documentation for more details [PyTorch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/).
✅ Expected Answer
gpu_test is currently the only FASRC partition with MIG (multi-instance GPU) mode enabled, so you must target a MIG instance explicitly. Get the GPU/MIG instance name with `nvidia-smi -L`, then set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the MIG instance, e.g. `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d`, or automate it with `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}')`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch
💬 Agent Messages (2)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?
🤖 Assistant Message #2
On the `gpu_test` partition, GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the physical card into smaller instances. To use the GPU correctly with PyTorch, you need to explicitly set the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable to the MIG instance ID. Here's what to do: 1. **Find the MIG instance name:** ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. **Set the environment variable:** ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-<your-instance-id> ``` Or automate it: ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This is **not required** on the regular `gpu` partition, only on `gpu_test`. See the PyTorch documentation for more details [PyTorch](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/).

Question 33: question_33

❓ Question

Our CryoSPARC master keeps coming up bound to the wrong machine name so the workers can't reach it. Where is that hostname actually set?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The hostname is set in the CryoSPARC Master configuration file: ``` cryosparc_master/config.sh ``` Specifically, look for the `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` variable in that file. For example: ```bash export CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME=holy_______.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` You'll need to: 1. Edit `config.sh` to set `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` to the correct hostname for the node where the master is running. 2. Restart the master with `cryosparcm restart` so the change takes effect. If you also changed the port, you may need to run `cryosparcm fixdbport` before restarting. See the FASRC CryoSPARC setup guide for more details: [CryoSPARC – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc)
✅ Expected Answer
It is set in the CryoSPARC master config file, `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, which holds the license, the path to the MongoDB database, the master hostname, and the base TCP port. The hostname of the node running the master application is typically hardcoded in that config.sh; if it is left unset, CryoSPARC takes the hostname of the machine on which `cryosparcm start` is called. If these config.sh settings are not correct you will experience errors. Note also that the TCP ports of the individual component programs are not individually configurable — only the base port the user connects to is.

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Running `cryosparcm start` brings up the following six applications: * **app** (CLI) * **command_core** * **command_rtp** * **command_vis** * **database** (MongoDB) * **webapp** These components communicate with one another over TCP. The base TCP port is configurable in `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, but individual ports for each component are not separately configurable [CryoSPARC – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc/).
✅ Expected Answer
Running `cryosparcm start` brings up six applications: app (cli), command_core, command_rtp, command_vis, database (MongoDB), and webapp. At the top level cryosparcm is a Supervisor-based shell script that manages them, and they mostly communicate with one another over TCP.

Question 35: question_35

❓ Question

Google finally approved my AlphaFold 3 request and sent me the weights as a .bin.zst download. Where am I supposed to unpack that on Cannon — can I just leave it in netscratch?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/alphafold_v3.def, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/run_alphafold_multi.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/alphapose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Short answer: **yes, you can unpack it in netscratch** — that's the right place for large, temporary model weights on Cannon. Here's the context: - **`/n/netscratch`** is FASRC's high-performance scratch filesystem. Each lab gets 50TB, and it's designed for transient, high-I/O data like model weights and intermediate outputs [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). - **Retention:** Files older than 90 days may be purged, especially if scratch is getting full [Scratch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). So don't treat it as permanent storage. - **No backups:** netscratch is not backed up [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage). ### Unpacking the `.bin.zst` file The `.zst` extension means it's compressed with **zstd**. You can unpack it with: ```bash zstd -d your_model_weights.bin.zst ``` Or, if you want to decompress in-place: ```bash zstd -d -f your_model_weights.bin.zst ``` ### Where exactly? A typical setup looks like: ``` /n/netscratch///alphafold3/ ``` You'd place the unpacked weights there, and then point your AlphaFold 3 Singularity/Podman container to that path (either via bind mounts or by specifying the model directory in your run script). ### A note on AlphaFold 3 on Cannon FASRC recommends running AlphaFold via **Singularity** containers on Cannon [AlphaFold – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold). The Singularity images themselves are stored in a cluster-wide location, so you don't need to manage those. You just need to make sure your model weights are accessible to the container (typically via bind mounts from netscratch). If you want, I can help you find the exact Singularity definition or run script for AlphaFold 3 on Cannon.
✅ Expected Answer
Extract the file Google sends you with `unzstd file.bin.zst`, then place the resulting `file.bin` in a lab share — do NOT put it in netscratch. That location is what you pass as `--model_dir` when you run AlphaFold3.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Question 36: question_36

❓ Question

Before I can run an AlphaFold3 prediction on the cluster, do I need to download the AlphaFold databases into my own lab storage first? If they're already there, what's the path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No, you do not need to download the AlphaFold databases yourself.** FASRC has already installed them in a cluster-wide location on SSD storage. ### Database paths - **AlphaFold 3:** `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3` - **AlphaFold 2:** `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2` These are mounted into your Singularity container via `--bind $data_dir:/data` in the example scripts, so the container can access them directly. ### What you *do* need to provide For AlphaFold 3, you must obtain the model parameters from Google yourself. After downloading the `.bin.zst` file, extract it with `unzstd` and place the resulting `.bin` file in a lab share (not netscratch). This becomes your `--model_dir` argument. ### Example usage The example scripts in the [FASRC User_Codes repo](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/) show how to bind the database directory and run AlphaFold 3 in two steps: 1. **Data pipeline** on a CPU partition 2. **Inference** on a GPU partition See the full documentation at [AlphaFold – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold/) for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
No download is needed. The AlphaFold database is kept in a cluster-wide location (on SSD, as the developers recommend), so individual users do not have to download it. The AlphaFold3 database is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3` (AlphaFold2's is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2`). Likewise, the Singularity images are cluster-wide at `/n/singularity_images/FAS/alphafold/`, so you do not have to copy those either.

Question 37: question_37

❓ Question

I copied a friend's sbatch script and stripped it down — it has no partition line, no memory line, and no time line. What does the scheduler actually hand me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/R/Example1/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/multicore.slurm, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/basicbatch.slurm, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you omit `#SBATCH --partition`, `#SBATCH --mem`, and `#SBATCH --time`, SLURM falls back to the cluster’s default values for each of those resources. The exact defaults depend on your cluster’s configuration, but here’s what typically happens: - **Partition**: If you don’t specify `--partition`, SLURM uses the **default partition** configured for your account/cluster. You can check what your default partition is by running `sinfo` or `sacctmgr show qos` to see which partitions are available and which are default. - **Memory**: If you don’t specify `--mem`, SLURM uses the **default memory allocation** per node or per core, as defined in the cluster’s SLURM configuration (often `slurm.conf`). This is usually a conservative default that may be far less than what you actually need. - **Time**: If you don’t specify `--time`, SLURM uses the **default time limit** for the partition (e.g., 24 hours or 7 days, depending on the cluster). This is often the maximum allowed for the default partition. ### How to check your actual defaults You can inspect the defaults by running: ```bash sinfo -o "%P %m %t" ``` This shows partition names, maximum memory per node, and maximum time limits. You can also check: ```bash sacctmgr show qos ``` to see QoS (Quality of Service) settings that may impose default limits. ### Recommendation It’s best practice to **explicitly specify** `--partition`, `--mem`, and `--time` in your scripts rather than relying on defaults, because: 1. Defaults can change without notice. 2. You may get less memory or a shorter time limit than you need, causing your job to fail or be killed prematurely. 3. It makes your script’s resource requirements clear to others (and to yourself when you revisit it later). If you want to share the script or tell me which cluster you’re using (e.g., O2, Odyssey, etc.), I can give more specific guidance on what defaults apply.
✅ Expected Answer
FASRC's defaults when nothing is requested are: 1 core, 100 MB of memory, 0 GPUs, and the `serial_requeue` partition. Time is the exception — there is no default time limit, so you must always declare a time yourself. The 100 MB memory default is very likely to get your job killed, so you should always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
💬 Agent Messages (1)
🤖 Assistant Message #1
When you omit `#SBATCH --partition`, `#SBATCH --mem`, and `#SBATCH --time`, SLURM falls back to the cluster’s default values for each of those resources. The exact defaults depend on your cluster’s configuration, but here’s what typically happens: - **Partition**: If you don’t specify `--partition`, SLURM uses the **default partition** configured for your account/cluster. You can check what your default partition is by running `sinfo` or `sacctmgr show qos` to see which partitions are available and which are default. - **Memory**: If you don’t specify `--mem`, SLURM uses the **default memory allocation** per node or per core, as defined in the cluster’s SLURM configuration (often `slurm.conf`). This is usually a conservative default that may be far less than what you actually need. - **Time**: If you don’t specify `--time`, SLURM uses the **default time limit** for the partition (e.g., 24 hours or 7 days, depending on the cluster). This is often the maximum allowed for the default partition. ### How to check your actual defaults You can inspect the defaults by running: ```bash sinfo -o "%P %m %t" ``` This shows partition names, maximum memory per node, and maximum time limits. You can also check: ```bash sacctmgr show qos ``` to see QoS (Quality of Service) settings that may impose default limits. ### Recommendation It’s best practice to **explicitly specify** `--partition`, `--mem`, and `--time` in your scripts rather than relying on defaults, because: 1. Defaults can change without notice. 2. You may get less memory or a shorter time limit than you need, causing your job to fail or be killed prematurely. 3. It makes your script’s resource requirements clear to others (and to yourself when you revisit it later). If you want to share the script or tell me which cluster you’re using (e.g., O2, Odyssey, etc.), I can give more specific guidance on what defaults apply.

Question 38: question_38

❓ Question

I have about 12,000 near-identical runs to do. Can I submit them as one --array job, and does that eat into the number of jobs I'm allowed to have queued?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/huce-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/rest_api.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/openapi_release_notes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can and should use a **single SLURM job array** for 12,000 near-identical runs. Here's what you need to know: ### 1. Yes, job arrays are designed for this A job array lets you submit one batch script with `--array=0-11999` (or similar), and SLURM will manage the 12,000 tasks as a single logical job. Each task gets its own index (`%a` or `$SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID`) so you can parameterize inputs/output files. See the [SLURM job array documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html) and FASRC's [Running Jobs: Job Arrays](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/#Job_arrays) guide. ### 2. Does it count against your queued-job limit? **Yes — but only as one job.** SLURM counts a job array as a **single job** toward your `MaxSubmitJobs` / `MaxJobsPerUser` limits ([Slurm job array support](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html)). So 12,000 array tasks submitted as one array consume only **1 queued job slot**, not 12,000. ### 3. Caveats & best practices - **MaxArraySize**: The cluster's `MaxArraySize` limits how many array tasks you can have at once. The default is 1,001, but the maximum supported is 4,000,001 ([Slurm job array support](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html)). Check your cluster's configured value with `scontrol show config | grep MaxArraySize` or ask FASRC support if 12,000 is allowed. - **Scheduler load**: FASRC recommends pausing **0.5–1 second** between `sbatch` submissions and ensuring each job runs at least **5–10 minutes** to avoid overwhelming the scheduler ([Submitting Large Numbers of Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/)). With a job array, SLURM handles scheduling internally, so this is less of a concern — but still keep jobs long enough to amortize scheduler overhead. - **Email notifications**: Avoid sending email for every array task; overwhelm the queue and jobs may be killed ([Submitting Large Numbers of Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/submitting-large-numbers-of-jobs/)). ### Quick example ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --array=0-11999 #SBATCH -p serial_requeue #SBATCH -t 0-2:00 #SBATCH --mem=2G INPUT_FILE="input_${SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID}.txt" OUTPUT_FILE="output_${SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID}.txt" your_command --input "$INPUT_FILE" --output "$OUTPUT_FILE" ``` If you'd like, I can help you check the cluster's `MaxArraySize` setting or refine your array script.
✅ Expected Answer
No — you must split it. The maximum array size is 10,000 (that cap applies to both the array index and the array size), so a single 12,000-element array is not allowed. And yes, it counts against your job cap: each array index counts as a single job for the purposes of the Maximum Number of Jobs per User, which is 10,100.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 39: question_39

❓ Question

My analysis takes about 3 hours every time on the shared partition, but when I run the exact same thing on serial_requeue it keeps getting killed for hitting the wall clock. What time should I be asking for there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your job takes about 3 hours on the shared partition, you should request **9 hours** on `serial_requeue`. The `serial_requeue` partition can place your job on substantially slower hardware. As a rule of thumb, allow for a factor of three variance in speed, so if your job takes 3 hours on most hardware, give it 9 hours on `serial_requeue` [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/).
✅ Expected Answer
Ask for roughly 9 hours. `serial_requeue` (and `gpu_requeue`) are mosaic partitions made of a wide variety of hardware, so run times vary a lot depending on which host you land on. The rule of thumb is a factor of three variance in speed — so a job that takes 3 hours on most hardware should be given 9 hours on `serial_requeue`. Alternatively, use the `--constraint` option to pin the job to a specific hardware type so the runtime is predictable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Question 40: question_40

❓ Question

My PI bought the nodes in our lab's own partition, and we only ever run there. So why does our fairshare keep dropping — aren't we just using hardware we already paid for?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/geophysics-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/huce-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
It's a common misconception, but on FASRC clusters, **Fairshare is a global quantity**, not a per-partition one. Here is why your score keeps dropping even if you only use your PI's private partition: * **Global Charging:** As the FASRC documentation explains, "We give RawShares to everyone that can be used anywhere on the cluster since Fairshare is a global quantity. Hence a user is charged regardless of what partition they use" [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). * **The Share vs. Usage Ratio:** Your Fairshare score is a ratio of your **Share** (what you are granted) versus your **Usage** (what you actually consume). Even if your PI bought the hardware for that partition, the system grants your lab a specific amount of "RawShares" based on that hardware. Every time you run a job, your usage goes up. If you are using your share of the cluster consistently, your Fairshare score will naturally drop because you are consuming the resources you were granted. * **The Goal of Fairshare:** The system is designed to ensure that labs who have *not* used their full resource grant get higher priority, while labs that have used more than their grant do not monopolize the cluster [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). In short, you are being "charged" for the compute time you consume, and that consumption is tracked globally across the entire cluster, regardless of which partition the nodes belong to.
✅ Expected Answer
Because Fairshare is a global quantity on Cannon, not a per-partition one: RawShares are granted to everyone and can be used anywhere on the cluster, so a user is charged regardless of which partition they run in. Groups that own private partitions are granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, and that grant exactly offsets the usage of the partition — so it nets out. The upside of this design is flexibility: because the Share is global, a group can leave its own partition idle and spend its shares elsewhere on the cluster instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 41: question_41

❓ Question

Last week a bunch of our jobs died from node failures and we cancelled several more that were misconfigured. Does that wasted compute still count against our lab's score, and how long until it stops hurting us?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_state_codes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, I cannot find specific information about how FASRC calculates or applies "compute scores" or how job cancellations and node failures affect them. The documentation covers job states (CANCELLED, FAILED, NODE_FAILURE) and SLURM behavior, but doesn't detail the scoring system or decay policies for wasted compute. To get an accurate answer about: 1. Whether cancelled/misconfigured jobs count against your lab's score 2. How long wasted compute affects your score 3. Any decay or reset policies I'd recommend reaching out to FASRC support directly at [fasrc@harvard.edu](mailto:fasrc@harvard.edu) or checking with your lab's FASRC coordinator, as they can provide details specific to your lab's allocation and the current scoring policies.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, it still counts. RawUsage is the sum of all usage including failed jobs, jobs that are requeued, and jobs that ran on nodes that failed — FASRC counts it because it is the user's responsibility to use the allocated time effectively even when it is cut short. That usage does decay, though: RawUsage is attenuated by the cluster's half-life, currently 3 days. Work done in the last 3 days counts at full cost, work done 6 days ago costs half, work done 9 days ago one fourth, and so on. To limit the damage from failures, FASRC recommends testing/verifying code before running and enabling checkpointing so jobs can restart where they left off.

Relatedly, under the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement the Researcher may disclose the Data only to PhD-seeking students of Harvard working under their supervision on the Research Project.
