Question 44: question_44

❓ Question

I want to spin up a few hundred cores on Cannon to crawl a public website faster. It's for academic research — any issue with that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-figure-out-how-efficient-my-job-is, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/training-materials, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasrc-ai-facilitation, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — there are significant issues with running web crawling/scraping on the Cannon cluster, even for academic research. **FASRC's Web Scraping Policy** [explicitly discourages using the cluster for scraping](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy/) because: - The cluster's size and ease of parallelization make it "easily weaponized," and your actions could negatively affect other researchers (e.g., IP blacklisting, rate-limiting, or legal action against Harvard). - You are representing Harvard, so your scraping activity is attributed to the university. - **You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the cluster.** - FASRC **highly prefers** that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster. **Key takeaways:** 1. **Don't run the crawler on Cannon.** Use a dedicated scraping environment (e.g., your own machine, a cloud instance, or a service designed for web crawling). 2. **Contact FASRC** before starting any scraping activity on the cluster — they need to be aware and may advise alternatives. 3. If the data involves sensitive or human subjects, it may also require compliance review before being placed on the cluster. For academic research, it's almost always better to acquire the data outside the HPC environment and then use Cannon for the actual analysis/computation.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, several. FASRC highly discourages using the cluster itself to scrape: because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers, so you should seek another avenue for data acquisition first. You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster. It is highly preferable that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing — and if the data is sensitive, confidential, contains human data, or it is unclear, doing it elsewhere is a requirement. Highly parallelized scraping is specifically called out as risking blacklisting of the cluster's or the university's IP range, which would harm other users. Being an academic does not exempt you from a site's usage policies.

Question 45: question_45

❓ Question

I logged in to Globus and picked the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection, but I can't browse into my home directory and the Everyone folder in my lab share doesn't show up either. Is my account misconfigured?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, your account is likely **not misconfigured**. Here's what's happening: 1. **Home directory not visible in Globus:** Globus access is typically granted to specific shared directories (like your Lab share), not your personal home directory by default. You may need to explicitly share your home directory or use a different method (like SSH/SCP) to access it. 2. **"Everyone" folder not showing up:** According to the documentation, the **Everyone** directory is **not available on Globus** and is intended only for internal sharing on the HPC cluster. This is expected behavior. **What you can do:** - Your **Lab** directory (the primary working directory) is shared via Globus by default, and folders within it are visible to the whole lab. - If you need to access your home directory, consider using SSH/SCP instead, or check if your PI/admin has explicitly shared it via Globus. - For the Everyone folder, you'll need to access it directly on the cluster (e.g., via SSH or Open OnDemand), not through Globus. If you're still having trouble accessing your Lab share via Globus, double-check that: - You've completed the Globus setup steps for FASRC users - Your PI/admin has granted you access to the specific lab share Would you like help with the Globus setup steps or alternative file transfer methods?
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is misconfigured — both are expected. Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances, because sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords could be shared inadvertently). And within a lab share, Globus can only see the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` folders — anything outside those, such as `Everyone` and `Transfer`, is not available through Globus (note the `Users` folder has been deprecated as of 2025). Also be aware symlinks do not work in Globus. If your lab share genuinely lacks a `Lab` directory or is not visible at all in the Holyoke/Boston collections, contact FASRC.

Question 46: question_46

❓ Question

I need to move a directory holding roughly half a million small output files off the cluster with Globus. What's the recommended way to package that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For a directory with ~500,000 small files, the recommended approach is to **create one or more `tar` archives** before transferring with Globus. Transferring hundreds of thousands of individual files is slow and error-prone; bundling them into archives is much more efficient. Here's the recommended workflow: ### 1. Create a file list (optional but helpful) ```bash cd /path/to/your/data find mydir/ -type f > file-list.txt ``` ### 2. Create a checksum file for integrity verification ```bash find mydir/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > checksums.shasum ``` This creates a SHA-1 checksum of all files so you can verify integrity after transfer. ### 3. Create tar archives Break the data into **manageable chunks** (e.g., 50–200 GB each) to reduce the risk of total data loss from corruption: ```bash tar -czf archive_part1.tar.gz -C /path/to/your/data mydir/ ``` If the data is large, consider splitting by subdirectories or using `split` after creating the tar. ### 4. Transfer with Globus Upload the resulting `.tar.gz` file(s) via Globus to your destination. ### Key considerations - **Keep archives reasonably sized** — very large single archives increase the risk of data loss from corruption. Breaking into smaller parts means a failure only loses a portion of your data. - **Checksums help verify integrity** after transfer, but only if the source data isn't modified between checksumming and archiving. - If you need to preserve special ACLs, capture them with `getfacl` before archiving. For more details, see the FASRC guide on [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/).
✅ Expected Answer
Tar the directory into subset files first. FASRC recommends that your tar files range in size from 1-100 GiB — that size range transfers more quickly (especially if the transfer is interrupted) and is smaller to retrieve if it ever needs to come back from Tape. If you leave hundreds or thousands of files in a single directory, Globus will go into an 'endpoint is too busy' state and your job will timeout, restart, timeout, etc. FASRC also recommends reading their 'Tips for using tar to archive data' page before tarring.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Question 47: question_47

❓ Question

I ran shasum on a folder before I tarred it up, and when I extracted the archive later and hashed it again the values don't match, even though every file looks fine. What am I doing wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The most common reason for this is that **the directory contents changed between when you ran the initial `shasum` and when you created the tar archive**. As noted in the FASRC documentation on archiving: > If the data in the directory is modified after you've run the checksum and before you've tar'd it, then the checksum will no longer match later when un-tar and compare later. If you need to tar an active filesystem, then checksum'ing will not be useful to you [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/). ### Other possible causes: 1. **Hidden/temporary files** — `shasum` on a folder (e.g., `shasum -r folder/`) may not have included all files (e.g., hidden files starting with `.`) depending on how you ran it, while `tar` captures everything. 2. **Symlinks** — `shasum` may follow or skip symlinks differently than `tar`, which by default preserves them. 3. **File permissions/timestamps** — While `shasum` only hashes file *contents*, `tar` preserves metadata. If extraction changes permissions or ownership, some tools may behave differently, though this wouldn't directly affect a content hash. ### Recommended approach: 1. **Generate the checksum *after* creating the tar**, not before: ```bash tar cf archive.tar folder/ shasum archive.tar > archive.tar.shasum ``` 2. **If you need to verify extracted contents**, generate the checksum of the *original* directory, then tar it, extract it, and compare: ```bash # Before archiving find folder/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > original.shasum tar cf archive.tar folder/ # After extraction find extracted_folder/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > extracted.shasum diff original.shasum extracted.shasum ``` This way, you're comparing like-for-like content hashes of the actual files, not the archive itself.
✅ Expected Answer
The checksum depends on the path you hand to find, so the same files hashed two different ways produce two different values. FASRC's guidance is to always do it the exact same way: cd to the directory *above* the one you are archiving (e.g. /n/mypath/scans) and then run find against the relative directory name (myscans) — not the full path. Two other things will break the comparison: (1) modifying the data after you took the checksum but before you tarred it (a checksum is useless on an active filesystem), and (2) storing the checksum file itself inside the tar, since its presence changes the checksum unless you remove it after un-tarring and before re-running.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving
💬 Agent Messages (3)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid

Question 48: question_48

❓ Question

RC made a tar of my project with Starfish. I copied it to netscratch and extracted it there, but instead of my ProjectX folder I got this weird nested tree of empty directories with ProjectX way down at the bottom. Is the archive corrupt?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the archive is not corrupt. This is expected behavior when tar files are created using Starfish's tagging system. When Starfish creates a tar archive, it captures the **entire original path** inside the tar file. So when you extract it, it recreates that full path relative to your current directory. For example, if you tarred `/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX` using Starfish, and then extracted it in `/n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/`, you'll find your data at: ``` /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX ``` **To avoid this**, you can: 1. **Extract to a specific directory** using the `-C` flag: ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar -C /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/ ``` This will place the contents directly in the specified directory. 2. **Strip the leading path** when extracting: ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar --strip-components=3 ``` (Adjust the number based on how many path components you want to remove.) See [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
The archive is fine. Starfish tars in place and captures the entire path inside the tar file, so extracting recreates that whole path underneath your output directory. Extracting a Starfish tar of /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX into /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp gives you /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX. Move the ProjectX directory to where you actually want it, then remove the leftover empty n/holylabs/jharvard_lab tree. Use plain `rm` rather than `rm -rf`, so it errors out if other files remain or you typed the wrong path.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

🤖 Assistant Message #2

Question 49: question_49

❓ Question

I already pushed a bunch of archives into our lab's tape area on Globus and then realized I named them badly. Can I just rename them, or move a couple into a different folder to tidy things up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes—you can rename and move files and folders inside your lab's tape staging area using the Globus File Manager, just as you would on any regular filesystem. Here's how: 1. **Log in to Globus** and open the **File Manager** ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)). 2. Navigate to your lab's tape collection (e.g., *"YourLab Research Lab Tape HU FASRC"*) and the disk-based staging/buffer area where your archives reside ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)). 3. **Rename files:** Select the file(s), then click the **Rename** action in the toolbar. 4. **Move files/folders:** Select the file(s) or folder(s), then click **Move** and choose the destination directory within the same collection. A couple of important notes: - **Permissions:** Read/write access applies to the whole lab tape collection on Globus—there's no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within it ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)). - **Symlinks:** Globus does not support symlinks, so avoid relying on them for organization ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)). - **Tape migration:** Remember that data in the staging area is eventually migrated to tape. If you've already triggered migration, the files may no longer be accessible for renaming/moving until they're recalled. If you run into issues or don't see the tape collection, your PI or General Manager may need to share it with you ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)).
✅ Expected Answer
No — do not delete, move, or rename data once it has been transferred to the tape buffer. The Globus buffer gives you write access to upload data destined for tape, and all changes within your lab's Globus collection are automatically picked up by the tape robot. Deletions on the Globus buffer may result in permanent deletion on tape, and deleting files that have already been placed on tape removes the 'link' and makes retrieval impossible. Mass deletions, moves, and file renaming require cumbersome manual intervention. Name files appropriately before you transfer them, and work with your PI or lab data manager on your lab's tape storage protocols.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Yes—you can rename and move files and folders inside your lab's tape staging area using the Globus File Manager, just as you would on any regular filesystem. Here's how: 1. **Log in to Globus** and open the **File Manager** ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)). 2. Navigate to your lab's tape collection (e.g., *"YourLab Research Lab Tape HU FASRC"*) and the disk-based staging/buffer area where your archives reside ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)). 3. **Rename files:** Select the file(s), then click the **Rename** action in the toolbar. 4. **Move files/folders:** Select the file(s) or folder(s), then click **Move** and choose the destination directory within the same collection. A couple of important notes: - **Permissions:** Read/write access applies to the whole lab tape collection on Globus—there's no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within it ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)). - **Symlinks:** Globus does not support symlinks, so avoid relying on them for organization ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)). - **Tape migration:** Remember that data in the staging area is eventually migrated to tape. If you've already triggered migration, the files may no longer be accessible for renaming/moving until they're recalled. If you run into issues or don't see the tape collection, your PI or General Manager may need to share it with you ([Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/)).

Question 50: question_50

❓ Question

Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, slurm.schedmd.com/fair_tree.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rclone

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's how to set up restricted Globus access for your postdoc: ## The Challenge Globus shares your **entire `Lab` directory** by default — there is no built-in Globus feature to restrict a shared collection to only one subfolder. As the docs note, "Globus can only see the folders `Lab` and `Users/$USER`" and "any folder must be inside `Lab`" ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)). ## Recommended Approach: Use POSIX Permissions + Globus Sharing Since **Globus uses your FASRC account's POSIX permissions** to determine what a user can see and access ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)), you can: 1. **Share the entire Lab collection via Globus** with your postdoc (they need to be added as a lab member on the cluster). 2. **Restrict POSIX permissions** on the folders your postdoc should **not** access, so they simply cannot see or enter those directories through Globus. ### Steps: 1. **Ensure the postdoc is a lab member** on the cluster (so they have a FASRC account). 2. **Share the Lab collection** with the postdoc via the Globus File Manager: - Navigate to the Lab collection - Go to the **Permissions** tab - Click **"Add Permissions — Share With"** - Enter the postdoc's Globus account email - Set permissions to **Read & Write** ([Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/)) 3. **Restrict access to other folders** using POSIX permissions: - Remove the postdoc's group membership from folders they shouldn't access, or - Set folder permissions so the postdoc's user/group has no read/execute access to those directories The postdoc will then only be able to navigate into and read/write the folders where they have POSIX permissions — effectively limiting their Globus access to just the one project folder. ## Alternative: Create a Dedicated Subfolder If the project folder is already a subdirectory of `Lab/`, you can: - Ensure the postdoc has read/write access only to that specific subfolder - Ensure they have **no read/execute permission** on sibling folders in `Lab/` This way, when they browse Globus, they can only enter the permitted folder. Would you like help with the specific `chmod`/`chown` commands for your folder structure?
✅ Expected Answer
You cannot. There is no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection — read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus. The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so FASRC cannot set those permissions; everyone with access is essentially a guest on the NESE tape system. (The PI and General Manager can add lab members to the collection, but only to all of it.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

How long can a job run on the FASSE H200 GPU nodes, and how many GPUs does each of those nodes have?

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation: - **Time limit:** Jobs on the `gpu_h200` partition (FASSE H200 nodes) have a **3-day** maximum run time [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). - **GPUs per node:** Each H200 node is equipped with **4 Nvidia H200 GPUs** [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/). When submitting a job, you request GPUs using `#SBATCH --gres=gpu:n` where `n` can be 1–4 per node [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/).
✅ Expected Answer

Question 53: question_53

❓ Question

A data provider will only let me download their dataset if I give them an IP address to add to their allowlist. What address do they see when I pull the data from a FASSE compute node?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pi-responsibilities-at-fas-rc

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you download data from a FASSE compute node, the external server will see the IP address of Harvard’s proxy server (**rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu**), not your compute node’s IP. All outbound internet traffic from FASSE goes through this proxy, so any allowlist you provide to a data provider should include the proxy’s IP address (or range) rather than individual compute node IPs. For reference, the proxy is configured as: - HTTP/HTTPS proxy: `http://rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu:3128` [FASSE Proxy Settings](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings) If you're unsure of the exact IP(s) assigned to `rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu`, you can resolve it from a FASSE node with `nslookup rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu` or `dig rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu`, or contact FASRC support for the current IP address(es) to share with the data provider.
✅ Expected Answer
140.247.232.228. All traffic to the Internet from FASSE — VPN, login, compute, or fasseood — goes out through the FASRC proxy (rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu), which currently has that external IP address. It is subject to change, so you can confirm it by running `curl icanhazip.com` from the node you will actually be working on.

Our data use agreement says the data has to be encrypted at rest. I was planning to stage the intermediate files for my FASSE jobs in netscratch since it's fast. Is that OK?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-information-on-storage-and-use-of-confidential-data-hrci, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/HDF5/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/rest.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mcs.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/HDF5/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to verify the encryption status of the `netscratch` filesystem to determine if it complies with your Data Use Agreement (DUA) requiring encryption at rest. It performed multiple searches across vector stores and local files using queries related to "netscratch encryption," "sensitive data," and "FASSE data transfers." However, the run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50 without retrieving a definitive policy statement confirming whether `netscratch` provides encryption at rest for intermediate files. **Answer:** Based on general Harvard FAS Research Computing (FASRC) policies, `netscratch` is a high-performance, non-persistent scratch space intended for temporary working data. It is **not** typically considered a secure storage location for sensitive or confidential data governed by strict Data Use Agreements (DUAs) unless specific encryption configurations are explicitly documented for that filesystem. Most scratch filesystems do not guarantee encryption at rest in the same way that designated secure storage volumes (like `netapp` or specific encrypted home directories) do. **Recommendation:** Do **not** stage DUA-protected intermediate files in `netscratch` unless you have explicit written confirmation from FAS Research Computing or Harvard Information Security that `netscratch` is encrypted at rest and approved for your specific data classification. Using it for sensitive data likely violates your DUA. Please consult the [FASRC Data Security documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/category/data-security/) or contact FASRC support directly to confirm the encryption status of `netscratch` or to identify an approved encrypted staging area.
✅ Expected Answer
No. If you have a DUA that requires encryption at rest, you must not use scratch for any data the DUA applies to — neither local scratch nor FASRC's global scratch supports encryption at rest. Separately, DSL 3 / FASSE data should not be stored in your home directory either. You are responsible for knowing and complying with the applicable Harvard Information Security Policy, the Harvard Research Data Security Policy, and any applicable contracts / data use agreements; if you are unsure how this applies to your data, consult FASRC at security@rc.fas.harvard.edu or your School Security Officer.

❓ Question

Our level 3 project storage doesn't show up as a collection in Globus at all. What do we have to do to get it added?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The agent attempted to resolve the issue by searching the knowledge base and local files for information regarding Globus collections, specifically looking for procedures to add "level 3 project storage" as a visible collection. The search queries included variations like "Globus collection not showing up" and "Globus collection." However, the retrieved documents primarily cover general Globus file transfer instructions, sharing permissions, and tape storage transfers, without providing specific steps for registering or exposing a "level 3 project storage" as a new collection in the Globus interface. Typically, adding a new storage system as a Globus collection is an administrative task performed by the storage provider (in this case, likely FAS Research Computing or the specific storage team) rather than an end-user action. Users generally cannot self-register new backend storage systems as Globus collections; they must be provisioned and published by the infrastructure team. **Note:** This run stopped after hitting the recursion limit of 50 iterations. **Answer:** You likely cannot add the "level 3 project storage" as a Globus collection yourself. Globus collections are typically managed and published by the storage infrastructure team (e.g., FAS Research Computing). To get this storage added, you should contact the **FAS Research Computing Support team** or the specific storage administrator for your project. They will need to provision the endpoint and publish it to the Globus service so it appears in your directory.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is broken: DSL 3 / FASSE storage is intentionally not included in Globus by default. To have it exposed, first review the governing DUA — Globus makes data available over the Internet without a VPN or private network, so a DUA requiring VPN/private-network access for all access to the data would not be Globus-compliant (consult your School Security Officer if unsure). Then submit a ticket to rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu that (1) includes the path to the project to add (e.g. /n/piname_project_l3), (2) states that the PI attests Globus is compliant with any contracts/DUAs governing the data, and (3) is either sent by the PI or receives a direct reply from the PI confirming it.

How much CPU and RAM am I actually allowed on the FASRC login server, and is there a cap on how many terminal windows I can have open at once?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, here's what you need to know about login node limits: ## CPU and Memory Limits on Login Nodes The Rocky 8 login nodes use a system-level mechanism called **cgroups** which limits each logged-in account to: - **1 CPU core** - **8 GB of memory** Any process that exceeds these limits will be killed without warning. Login nodes are meant as gateways for launching and monitoring jobs, and for running light processes to prepare your jobs or environment—not for actual computation. If you need more resources, you should use an [interactive session](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/#Interactive_jobs_and_salloc) on the compute nodes instead. ## Terminal Window Limits The documentation doesn't specify a hard cap on the number of terminal windows you can have open at once. However, since each terminal session counts as a logged-in account subject to the cgroup limits above, opening many terminal windows could potentially trigger resource limits if you're running processes in them. For more details, see the [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities/) page.
✅ Expected Answer
Each login-node session is limited to 1 core and 8GB of memory, and you may have at most 5 sessions per user. Users who abuse the login nodes may have their sessions terminated, and the login nodes are rebooted during monthly maintenance to clear out stale sessions. If you need more than 5 sessions, switch to batch jobs, use Open OnDemand (which gives you a desktop with multiple terminals on a dedicated compute node), or use screen/tmux to multiplex one session.

Question 57: question_57

❓ Question

I ran ssh-copy-id and my key works great when I'm in the office, but from my apartment it still makes me punch in the 6-digit code every single time. Did the key not install properly?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-key-error, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your key was likely installed correctly — the issue is that **SSH key authentication alone is not enough from external networks**. According to the FASRC documentation on terminal access: > Passwordless SSH is permitted to the Cannon login nodes from Harvard networks (including VPNs). **If you are on an external network you are still required to provide Two Factor Authentication.** So when you're in the office (on a Harvard network), your SSH key authenticates you without a password. But from your apartment (an external network), even with your key properly installed, you still need to provide the 6-digit OpenAuth token code as part of the two-factor authentication requirement. This is expected behavior — your key is working fine; the 2FA prompt is a security requirement for external connections, not a sign that the key failed to install. If you want to verify your key is properly installed, you can check that it appears in `~/.ssh/authorized_keys` on the cluster, or try connecting from a Harvard network to confirm passwordless login works there.
✅ Expected Answer
The key is fine — this is by design. Passwordless (ssh-key) authentication is only permitted to the Cannon login nodes from Harvard networks, including Harvard/FASRC VPNs. If you connect from an external network you are still required to provide two-factor authentication regardless of your key. So either connect to the VPN from home, or accept the 2FA prompt. (On FASSE, passwordless ssh is permitted from the FASSE VPN only.) You can confirm the key installed by checking that it is present in ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the cluster.

Question 58: question_58

❓ Question

I'm an IQSS researcher and my survey data is classified Level 2. Which cluster am I supposed to be on, and what exactly goes in the VPN box versus the cluster login box?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasse-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/other-institutions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-levels

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
There are two important clarifications for your situation: 1. **Cluster for Level 2 data:** FASSE is specifically for **Level 3** data, not Level 2. For Level 2 data, you would typically use a different cluster (not FASSE). You should check the [Data Security Levels](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-levels/) page to confirm which cluster is appropriate for Level 2 data. 2. **VPN vs. Cluster Login Boxes:** Based on the [IQSS FASSE Quickstart Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasse-quickstart-guide/), here's how the login works: - **VPN box (Cisco AnyConnect):** Enter your FASSE username in the format `username@fasse` (with the "@fasse" suffix), your FASSE password, and your FASSE 2FA token. - **Cluster login box (FASSE OOD portal):** Enter just your FASRC username **without** the "@fasse" suffix, and your FASRC password. Since your data is Level 2, I'd recommend confirming with your IQSS partners or contacting FASRC directly to determine which cluster you should actually be using, as FASSE is designated for Level 3 data only.
✅ Expected Answer
Level 2 data belongs on Cannon, the FAS research computing cluster for Data Security Level 2 (only Level 3 data must move to the FASSE secure environment). To get on: in the Cisco AnyConnect VPN client use server address vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu and enter your FASRC username in the format username@fasrc, your FASRC password, and your FASRC 2FA token — note this is NOT the code associated with your HarvardKey account. Then log into the Cannon cluster at https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu using just your FASRC username WITHOUT the '@fasrc', plus your FASRC password.

Question 59: question_59

❓ Question

My pandas script keeps blowing up with a memory error because the file is bigger than the node's RAM. Does FASRC want me to rewrite it, or can I keep using pandas somehow?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/Example2/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Short answer: **You don’t have to rewrite from scratch**, but you do need to change *how* you load/process the data so it fits in memory. Pandas itself is an in-memory library, so if your file is larger than the node’s RAM, you’ll need to either: 1. **Process the data in chunks** using pandas’ built-in chunking, or 2. **Use a library designed for out-of-core/larger-than-memory data**, such as **Dask** or **Polars**. Here are your options: ### Option 1: Chunked pandas You can read large files in chunks with `pd.read_csv(..., chunksize=...)` and process each chunk separately. This keeps memory usage bounded. ### Option 2: Dask [Dask](https://docs.dask.org/) provides a pandas-like API that can handle datasets larger than RAM by processing data in parallel and out-of-core. It’s a drop-in replacement for many pandas workflows. ### Option 3: Polars [Polars](https://pola.rs/) is a fast, memory-efficient DataFrame library that can handle larger datasets more efficiently than pandas. ### What FASRC expects FASRC doesn’t require you to abandon pandas, but they do expect you to: - **Request appropriate memory** via SLURM (`--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`) that matches your job’s needs - **Use memory-efficient approaches** (chunking, out-of-core libraries) when your data exceeds available RAM - **Avoid loading entire large files into memory** when unnecessary ### Next steps 1. Estimate how much memory your script needs (try a small subset first) 2. Request that memory in your SLURM job script using `--mem` 3. If the file still won’t fit, switch to chunked processing or Dask/Polars Would you like help with any of these approaches, or do you need help estimating memory requirements for your specific file?
✅ Expected Answer
Both options exist, and which one FASRC recommends depends on scale. Start with chunking. For moderately large datasets, Dask can enhance Pandas by parallelizing operations — you can convert a Pandas DataFrame to a Dask DataFrame with dd.from_pandas() and keep most of your existing code. But for very large datasets that exceed memory constraints, using Dask alone as a substitute for Pandas is the more effective solution: Dask is a drop-in replacement with a familiar DataFrame API that works lazily, breaking work into smaller tasks that only execute when you call .compute(). Separately, switching from CSV to a columnar format like Parquet (e.g. df.to_parquet('output_data.parquet', compression='snappy')) greatly improves I/O performance.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 60: question_60

❓ Question

I switched to the newest Intel module on the cluster and now the compiler command I've always used doesn't exist anymore. What am I supposed to call to build my C file now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/Armadillo/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you switch to the newest Intel module (OneAPI-based, such as `intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`), the old `icc` command may no longer be available or may behave differently. According to the documentation, the OneAPI Intel compiler suite uses **`icx`** as the new C compiler command instead of the legacy `icc`. You can verify which compiler is available by running: ```bash $ which icx ``` To compile your C file, use: ```bash $ icx your_file.c -o your_program ``` If you need to stick with the legacy `icc` command, you may need to load an older Intel module version instead of the OneAPI-based one. For more details on C compilation on the cluster, see the [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/) guide.
✅ Expected Answer
Intel renamed the compiler binaries at version 24.0.0. For Intel modules below 24.0.0 the commands are icc (C), ifortran (Fortran), and icpc (C++). For Intel 24.0.0 and above they are icx (C), ifx (Fortran), and icpx (C++). So with a newer Intel module you must invoke icx instead of icc. (You can list the available Intel compilers with `module spider intel` and load a specific one, e.g. `module load intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 61: question_61

❓ Question

Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, that's not recommended. According to the FASRC documentation: 1. **Don't compile on login nodes** — It's best practice to compile on a compute node (via an interactive job) rather than right after SSH-ing into a login node, since compilation can take seconds to a minute depending on complexity [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/). 2. **Don't put the gcc line in your sbatch script** — You should compile separately and then use the generated executable in your production batch job. Including the compilation command in the `sbatch` script will recompile the program every time you submit the job [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/). **The recommended workflow is:** 1. Compile separately on a compute node (e.g., using `salloc` or `srun --pty`) 2. Test the executable 3. Then submit your production run as a batch job using the pre-compiled executable If you make changes to the source code, recompile separately again, then submit the batch job with the updated executable.
✅ Expected Answer
No on both counts. FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile a C program (compilation can take a few seconds to a minute depending on the code's complexity), and specifically recommends using the `test` partition to compile and test the program before running the production job. Best practice is to compile the code separately and then use the resulting executable in your sbatch script — avoid putting the compilation command inside the sbatch script, because that recompiles the program on every submission. If you change the source, recompile separately and then resubmit the production batch job.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 62: question_62

❓ Question

I want to completely wipe my Julia install off the cluster and start clean. Is there a single command, and does it leave junk behind in my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/Notebook.md, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
There isn't a single "uninstall" command for a user-installed Julia on the cluster. The standard installation (via `curl -fsSL https://install.julialang.org | sh`) drops the Julia binaries into your `$HOME` directory and adds a `juliaup` initialization line to your `~/.bashrc` [Julia Programming Language – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia/). To start clean, you'll need to do two things: 1. **Remove the Julia binaries and related files** from your home directory. The installer places files in locations like `~/.julia` (packages, caches, registries) and the Julia executable itself. You can remove these with: ```bash rm -rf ~/.julia rm -rf ~/.juliaup ``` (Adjust paths if your installation placed them elsewhere.) 2. **Clean up `~/.bashrc`** by removing the `juliaup` initialization line that was added to your `$PATH`. Open `~/.bashrc` in an editor and delete the line(s) that source or initialize `juliaup`. After doing this, Julia will no longer be available in your shell, and the associated junk (packages, caches, etc.) will be gone. If you also installed Julia packages via `Pkg.add()`, those are stored in `~/.julia`, so removing that directory handles them. Would you like help locating the exact lines in `~/.bashrc` to remove?
✅ Expected Answer
Run `juliaup self uninstall`. That is the uninstall command, but it does not always clean everything up: afterward, check for the `~/.julia` and `~/.juliaup` directories and delete them manually if they are still there. (This matters because the default Julia install location is $HOME, and the installer also adds Juliaup initialization to your ~/.bashrc to put `julia` on your $PATH.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia
💬 Agent Messages (3)
🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: I want to completely wipe my Julia install off the cluster and start clean. Is there a single command, and does it leave junk behind in my home directory?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
There isn't a single "uninstall" command for a user-installed Julia on the cluster. The standard installation (via `curl -fsSL https://install.julialang.org | sh`) drops the Julia binaries into your `$HOME` directory and adds a `juliaup` initialization line to your `~/.bashrc` [Julia Programming Language – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia/). To start clean, you'll need to do two things: 1. **Remove the Julia binaries and related files** from your home directory. The installer places files in locations like `~/.julia` (packages, caches, registries) and the Julia executable itself. You can remove these with: ```bash rm -rf ~/.julia rm -rf ~/.juliaup ``` (Adjust paths if your installation placed them elsewhere.) 2. **Clean up `~/.bashrc`** by removing the `juliaup` initialization line that was added to your `$PATH`. Open `~/.bashrc` in an editor and delete the line(s) that source or initialize `juliaup`. After doing this, Julia will no longer be available in your shell, and the associated junk (packages, caches, etc.) will be gone. If you also installed Julia packages via `Pkg.add()`, those are stored in `~/.julia`, so removing that directory handles them. Would you like help locating the exact lines in `~/.bashrc` to remove?

Question 63: question_63

❓ Question

I chmod 777'd a file in my lab's share so a collaborator in a different lab group could grab it, but they still get permission denied when they try to read it. What's going on?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unix-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue is almost certainly **directory permissions**, not the file itself. In Unix/POSIX permissions, **every directory in the path** to a file must grant at least **execute (x)** permission to the user trying to access it. This is often called "traverse" permission. Here's what's likely happening: 1. **The file itself** has `777` permissions (read/write/execute for everyone), so the file-level permissions are fine. 2. **But the parent directory** (or one of its ancestors up to the share root) likely has permissions like `drwxr-x---` or `drwxr-s---`, which means **`Other` has no access** to that directory. 3. Even though the file is world-readable, your collaborator can't even *see* or *traverse* into the directory to reach the file. **To fix this:** - Check the permissions on the directory containing the file: ```bash ls -ld /path/to/directory ``` - If the directory shows `---` or `-x` for `Other`, you need to grant at least **execute** permission to `Other` on that directory: ```bash chmod o+x /path/to/directory ``` - You may need to do this for **every directory in the path** from the share root down to the file. ⚠️ **Note:** For FASSE/L3 shares, FASRC policy explicitly states these should **never** allow `Other` access. If you're on an FASSE/L3 share, `chmod 777` won't work for cross-lab sharing — you'll need to use **Globus** or another approved file-sharing method instead [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). For more detail on how permissions work, see [Understanding file and group permissions](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions/).
✅ Expected Answer
Making the file world-readable is not enough on its own. For a user in the 'Other' category to reach a file, they must first be able to traverse into the top-level share/directory that contains it. If the top level of the lab directory or share is set drwxr-x---, drwxrwx---, or drwxr-s--- then Other has no access to the share at all, even if a file inside it is set -rwxrwxrwx. Other must first be able to 'see' into the directory/share; otherwise it has no access. (FASSE/L3 shares should never allow Other access.) To share with someone outside the group, the enclosing directory must permit Other traversal - e.g. the 'Everyone' directory that lab shares provide for exactly this purpose - or the person can be added to your lab Group by your PI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Question 64: question_64

❓ Question

My labmate keeps his personal scratch folder under Users/ in our old lab share, but our newly created share and netscratch have no Users folder. Where am I supposed to put my own personal directory now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here's what you need to know: ## Old vs. New Lab Share Structure In the **old lab shares**, it was common to see a `Users/` subdirectory where individual lab members would create personal folders (e.g., `/n/old_lab/Users/username/`). This was an informal convention, not a standardized FASRC structure. In the **new lab shares** (part of the Storage Modernization Initiative), the directory structure is different. According to the [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/) documentation, each lab receives a **Lab Directory** (4 TiB) that is shared among all lab members. The recommended structure includes: - **Lab/** – The primary working directory, shared with the whole lab and accessible via Globus. Individual users can create subdirectories here. - **Everyone/** – A directory visible to any HPC user, intended for cross-lab collaboration (not available via Globus). ## Where to Put Your Personal Directory You have a few options: 1. **Create a personal subdirectory under your Lab directory** (e.g., `/n/new_lab/Lab/username/`). This is the most common approach for personal lab data that still needs to be accessible to your group. 2. **Use your Home Directory** – Each FASRC account comes with a **100 GiB Home Directory** for individual use. This is the most appropriate place for truly personal files that don't need to be shared with the lab. 3. **Use netscratch** – If you need high-performance temporary storage for active computation, use `/n/netscratch`. Note that netscratch is **not backed up** and files older than 90 days may be purged [Scratch Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch). ## Recommendation If you need a personal folder that's still visible to your labmates, create one under your Lab directory. If you need truly personal, private storage, use your Home Directory. If you need high-performance scratch for active jobs, use netscratch. For more details on locating your new storage folders, see the [Getting Started with FAS RC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
The Users directory only exists on shares created prior to October 2024. On new shares and on /n/netscratch there is no Users directory - FASRC says to use the Lab directory to create personal folders instead. The Lab directory is set drwxrws--- so that every member of the lab group has read/write/execute access to share data amongst the group, and it is the preferred place both for personal folders on new shares and for sharing data with your lab group. On the older shares, the sub-directories in the Users directory are owned by individuals and by default only allow execute access to the group.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

❓ Question

I log into the FASRC web portal in my browser, it accepts my credentials, and then it just spins forever and never shows the menus. Restarting the browser doesn't help. What can I do myself before emailing support?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/get-a-fasse-account-and-project-group, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mcz-staff-compute, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, the indexed FASRC documentation does not appear to contain specific troubleshooting steps for a portal that accepts credentials but then "spins forever" without loading menus. This is a browser-side issue that may not be covered in the current knowledge base. However, here are some general self-help steps you can try before emailing support: 1. **Clear your browser cache and cookies** for the portal site — stale or corrupted cookies are a common cause of login loops. 2. **Try a different browser** (or an incognito/private window) to rule out browser extensions or cached data interfering. 3. **Check for browser console errors** (usually via F12 → Console tab) — any JavaScript errors may give a clue. 4. **Ensure you're using a supported browser** — check the portal's login page for any noted browser requirements. 5. **Try a different network** — if you're on campus Wi-Fi or behind a firewall, it could be interfering with the portal's resources. If none of these resolve the issue, you'll likely need to email FASRC support with details about your browser, any error messages you see in the console, and when the problem started.
✅ Expected Answer
If the browser hangs for a long time after authenticating without displaying the Open OnDemand dashboard, restart your Per-User NGINX (PUN) process by visiting: - Cannon: https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ - FASSE: https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ Also note that the very first login can legitimately take some time while OOD sets up files and cache in your home directory; that Avast security software can cause 'no internet', 'read error', and 'the requested URL could not be retrieved' messages (disable Web Shield); and that Chrome or Firefox are recommended over Safari and Internet Explorer.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop

Question 67: question_67

❓ Question

My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/EP/Example1/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scrun.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/OpenMP/Example2/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The key to solving this is to **read the number of CPUs Slurm allocated to your job** from the environment and use that value to set your parallel backend. ### The Environment Variable When you request CPUs in your SLURM script (e.g., `#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8`), SLURM sets the environment variable **`SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`** to that number. ### How to use it in R You can read this variable inside your R script and pass it to your parallel backend (e.g., `parallel`, `future`, or `foreach`). **Example using the `parallel` package:** ```R # Get the number of CPUs allocated by SLURM n_cores <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) # If the variable is empty (e.g., running interactively), default to 1 if (is.na(n_cores) || n_cores == 0) { n_cores <- 1 } # Create a cluster with exactly that many workers cl <- makeCluster(n_cores) # ... run your parallel code ... stopCluster(cl) ``` **Example using the `future` package:** ```R library(future) plan(multisession, workers = as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK", "1"))) ``` ### Why this matters If you hardcode a large number of workers (e.g., `detectCores()` or a fixed number like 32) regardless of what SLURM allocated, you will oversubscribe the node. This causes the "grinding to a halt" you described because the OS spends more time context-switching between threads than doing useful work. By tying your worker count to `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`, you ensure your job respects the resources it was granted. ### Checklist for your SLURM script 1. **Request the correct number of CPUs:** ```bash #SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8 ``` 2. **Ensure your R script reads `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`.** 3. **Avoid calling `detectCores()`** without filtering, as it may report all physical cores on the node rather than the subset allocated to your job.
✅ Expected Answer
Do not use parallel::detectCores() to determine the number of CPUs/processes to use in your job - it detects the number of physical CPU cores on the compute node, not the number allocated to your job, which is why you end up oversubscribed. Use parallelly::availableCores() instead, which returns the number of CPUs allocated to your Slurm job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel
💬 Agent Messages (12)

Args: My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?

🤖 Assistant Message #12
The key to solving this is to **read the number of CPUs Slurm allocated to your job** from the environment and use that value to set your parallel backend. ### The Environment Variable When you request CPUs in your SLURM script (e.g., `#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8`), SLURM sets the environment variable **`SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`** to that number. ### How to use it in R You can read this variable inside your R script and pass it to your parallel backend (e.g., `parallel`, `future`, or `foreach`). **Example using the `parallel` package:** ```R # Get the number of CPUs allocated by SLURM n_cores <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) # If the variable is empty (e.g., running interactively), default to 1 if (is.na(n_cores) || n_cores == 0) { n_cores <- 1 } # Create a cluster with exactly that many workers cl <- makeCluster(n_cores) # ... run your parallel code ... stopCluster(cl) ``` **Example using the `future` package:** ```R library(future) plan(multisession, workers = as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK", "1"))) ``` ### Why this matters If you hardcode a large number of workers (e.g., `detectCores()` or a fixed number like 32) regardless of what SLURM allocated, you will oversubscribe the node. This causes the "grinding to a halt" you described because the OS spends more time context-switching between threads than doing useful work. By tying your worker count to `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`, you ensure your job respects the resources it was granted. ### Checklist for your SLURM script 1. **Request the correct number of CPUs:** ```bash #SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8 ``` 2. **Ensure your R script reads `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`.** 3. **Avoid calling `detectCores()`** without filtering, as it may report all physical cores on the node rather than the subset allocated to your job.

Question 68: question_68

❓ Question

I had openmpi loaded, then loaded mpich later in the same session, and now openmpi is gone from my module list. Did I corrupt my environment, and can I keep both around?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example4/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example2/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example3/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example5/build.sh

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you haven't corrupted your environment. This is expected behavior with software modules. On the FASRC cluster, MPI implementations are managed as mutually exclusive modules. When you load `mpich`, it replaces the previously loaded `openmpi` module because both provide the same MPI interface libraries and executables (like `mpicc`, `mpirun`). You cannot have both loaded simultaneously in the same session. **To use OpenMPI again**, simply unload mpich and load openmpi: ```bash module unload mpich module load openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01 ``` Or, if you're unsure of the exact version, you can check available versions with: ```bash module spider openmpi ``` **Key points:** - There is no default MPI library loaded at login — you must explicitly choose one - The cluster offers both **OpenMPI** and **Mpich**, each compiled with either the GNU or Intel compiler suite - You need to load the appropriate compiler module alongside your MPI module (e.g., `module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01`) For more details, see the [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/) documentation.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is corrupted - this is intentional. The modules are set up so that you can only have one MPI module loaded at a time; if you try loading a second one it will automatically unload the first, which is done to avoid dependency collisions. So you cannot keep both loaded: pick one implementation (OpenMPI or MPICH, each built with either the GNU or the Intel compiler suite) and load it with its matching compiler module, e.g. module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01. Note also that there is no default MPI library in your environment when you log in - you must load one.

Question 69: question_69

❓ Question

I need mpi4py in a conda environment, but it has to be built against the same OpenMPI my Fortran code uses. I just ran mamba create with mpi4py in the package list - what MPI did that actually give me, and how do I get OpenMPI instead?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, slurm.schedmd.com/mpi_guide.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/Example1/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you ran `mamba create ... mpi4py` without specifying an MPI implementation, **conda installed MPICH by default** as the underlying MPI library for `mpi4py` [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/). If your Fortran code uses **OpenMPI**, you need to rebuild `mpi4py` against that same OpenMPI. Here's how: 1. **Load the desired MPI module** (and a matching compiler): ```bash module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01 module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03 ``` 2. **Load Python**: ```bash module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01 ``` 3. **Create a conda environment *without* mpi4py**: ```bash mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel source activate python3_env2 ``` 4. **Install mpi4py with pip** (so it picks up the loaded OpenMPI): ```bash pip install mpi4py ``` This approach ensures `mpi4py` is compiled against the same OpenMPI version your Fortran code uses [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/). Want help checking which OpenMPI version your Fortran code is linked against?
✅ Expected Answer
Putting mpi4py in the mamba create package list (e.g. mamba create -n python3_env1 python numpy pip wheel mpi4py) also installs an MPI library required by mpi4py, and by default that is MPICH - not OpenMPI. If your workflow requires a specific flavor and/or version of MPI, install mpi4py with pip into a custom conda environment instead: load the compiler and MPI modules first (module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01; module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03), load a Python module (module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01), create the environment WITHOUT mpi4py (mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel), activate it (source activate python3_env2), then run pip install mpi4py so it builds against the loaded OpenMPI.

Question 70: question_70

❓ Question

I used the 'copy mount path to clipboard' button in Starfish and got /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab, but when I cd to it on a login node I get 'No such file or directory'. What's the correct path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The path `/n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab` is a **network mount path** used by Starfish and other GUI tools — it is **not accessible directly from login or compute nodes**. On FASRC clusters, the correct path to your lab directory is: ``` /n/holylabs/C/jharvard_lab ``` The difference is that Starfish shows the VAST-mounted path (`/n/vast-holylabs/...`), but on the cluster itself, the lab storage is mounted at `/n/holylabs/...` [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). You can verify this by running: ```bash ls /n/holylabs/C/jharvard_lab ``` If you need to access this storage from your local desktop, note that holylabs **cannot be mounted via SMB** on a desktop — you'll need to use SCP, SFTP, or Globus instead [Mounting Storage on Desktop or Laptop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
The path Starfish copies is not directly usable - you must make two edits: remove 'vast-' and remove '/C'. So /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab becomes /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab. The letter C indicates the filesystem is on the Cannon cluster; a group share on FASSE would show F instead of C before the group name.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 71: question_71

❓ Question

I want to drag a few files out of my home directory into my lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface, but my home directory doesn't show up as an endpoint in Globus. What else can I use?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your home directory isn't available as a Globus endpoint, you can use the **Open OnDemand Remote Desktop** interface to drag and drop files between your home directory and your lab's netscratch folder. Here's how: 1. Go to the Open OnDemand dashboard: - Cannon: [https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard](https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard) - FASSE: [https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard](https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard) 2. Start a **Remote Desktop** session (see the [Remote Desktop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vdi-apps/#Remote_Desktop) documentation for details). 3. Once the session starts, open a **File Manager** by clicking the "Home" folder on the Desktop (or via **Applications → File Manager**). 4. Navigate to your **source** folder (e.g., your home directory). 5. Open **another File Manager window**. 6. Navigate to your **destination** folder (e.g., your lab's netscratch folder). 7. You should now have two file manager windows side-by-side, allowing you to drag and drop files between them. This approach gives you a point-and-click interface for transferring files between directories that aren't Globus endpoints.
✅ Expected Answer
Globus does not have access to home directories, which is why yours does not appear. For transfers to or from your home directory, use an Open OnDemand Remote Desktop session instead: go to the OOD dashboard (Cannon: rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, FASSE: fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu), start a Remote Desktop session, open a File Manager by clicking the 'Home' folder on the desktop (or Applications -> File Manager), open a second File Manager window, navigate one to the source (e.g. /n/home01/jharvard/) and the other to the destination (e.g. /n/netscratch/jharvard_lab/Lab/), then right-click the source file -> Copy and right-click the destination window -> Paste (drag-and-drop works too, but behaves like cut-and-paste). Command-line tools (cp, mv, rsync, fpsync) are the alternative; Globus is only recommended for transfers between different filesystems, not within one.

Question 72: question_72

❓ Question

My collaborator is at another university and doesn't have a HarvardKey. Can she still get an account on the cluster, and is there anything I have to keep doing so her access doesn't get shut off?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, your collaborator can get an account even without a HarvardKey. According to the [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) page: - **If she is a faculty member at another university** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should select **EXTERNAL** when requesting an account. - **If she is a student or researcher at another university or company** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should also select **EXTERNAL**. ### What you need to keep doing The account is sponsored by a Harvard PI (you, in this case). While the documentation doesn't specify a recurring action you must take to keep her access active, the policies note that: 1. **All account holders agree to Harvard's Electronic Access and Information Security policies** ([Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/)). 2. **All active FAS RC account holders are subscribed to the notifications mailing list**, which is a requirement for all users. 3. **Cluster access is intended for legitimate research purposes** benefiting Harvard University research ([Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/)). If her access is at risk of being shut off, it would likely be due to inactivity, policy violations, or if the sponsoring PI's arrangement with FASRC changes. For specific details on account maintenance or deactivation policies, you may want to contact FASRC directly or check the [Acceptable Use](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use/) guidelines.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes. A collaborator without a HarvardKey can apply for an external FASRC account through the account request portal (https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). External accounts have to be re-enabled every 90 days: the PI must request an extension every 90 days or the account will be suspended. Faculty can sponsor FASRC accounts for anyone working in their lab, including external collaborators.

Question 73: question_73

❓ Question

I'm a new PI with a HarvardKey but the system won't let me approve my grad student's FASRC account request. What do I need to do?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding
